It just kind of sits there, even when nothing bad is actually going on.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: It's been a while now. This has all been going on since the robbery at that festival job I was working... that was about two years and three months ago.\n\nDoctor: Do you have unwanted memories of the robbery that keep coming back?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. It doesn't take much, really. Sometimes just hearing a cash box snap shut, or... or having somebody come up behind me in a kitchen, it brings the robbery back so fast. I can actually hear the voices again, and my stomach just drops, like it's happening to me right then.\n\nDoctor: Do you go out of your way to avoid reminders of the robbery?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I definitely do. I mean, I go out of my way to turn down any festival jobs now, or... or evening deliveries. Even certain shared kitchens, I just won't go near them. I just really don't want to think about that night, or feel that panic start up all over again, so it's easier to just stay away.\n\nDoctor: Since the robbery, have you felt emotionally cut off from other people?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I have. I mean, I still answer my friends sometimes when they reach out, but... when I'm actually with them, I just feel like I'm watching everything from far away. I just don't really know how to jump back into being close with them like I was before.\n\nDoctor: Since the robbery, have you been unusually jumpy or easily startled?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, I don't think I'm any more jumpy than I used to be. I haven't noticed anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Since the robbery, have you felt constantly on guard or watchful, even when you were safe?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I'd say so. Like, whenever I'm in a parking lot or at a pickup spot, I'm just... always checking who is near me. I make sure I know exactly where my car is and figure out how fast I could get out of there if I needed to. Even when everyone else around me seems completely relaxed and fine, I'm just constantly keeping an eye on things.\n\nDoctor: Do you have nightmares about the robbery?\n\nPatient: No, I don't really have nightmares about it. I mean, I might have regular dreams sometimes, but nothing specifically about the robbery that wakes me up in a panic or anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Since the robbery, have you been blaming yourself for what happened?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. I just... I keep telling myself I should have known better than to take that booking in the first place. And I feel like I failed the people who were helping me by not keeping them safe. It's hard not to feel like it's my fault.\n\nDoctor: Since the robbery, have you found yourself believing that the world is unsafe or that you can’t trust people?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, I know bad things can happen, obviously, but I don't walk around thinking everyone is out to get me or that the whole world is completely unsafe. I still trust the people I know, and I don't think everyone is bad just because of what those guys did.\n\nDoctor: When something reminds you of the robbery, does your body react strongly—like your heart pounding, sweating, shaking, or feeling short of breath?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't really noticed anything like that. I mean, my heart doesn't start pounding or anything, and I don't get sweaty or start shaking. Physically, I just feel normal when it comes to mind.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. I just don't really know how to jump back into being close with them like I was before.\n\nDoctor: Since the robbery, have you been unusually jumpy or easily startled?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, I don't think I'm any more jumpy than I used to be. I haven't noticed anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Since the robbery, have you felt constantly on guard or watchful, even when you were safe?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I'd say so. Like, whenever I'm in a parking lot or at a pickup spot, I'm just... always checking who is near me. I make sure I know exactly where my car is and figure out how fast I could get out of there if I needed to. Even when everyone else around me seems completely relaxed and fine, I'm just constantly keeping an eye on things.\n\nDoctor: Do you have nightmares about the robbery?\n\nPatient: No, I don't really have nightmares about it. I mean, I might have regular dreams sometimes, but nothing specifically about the robbery that wakes me up in a panic or anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Since the robbery, have you been blaming yourself for what happened?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. I just... I keep telling myself I should have known better than to take that booking in the first place. And I feel like I failed the people who were helping me by not keeping them safe. It's hard not to feel like it's my fault.\n\nDoctor: Since the robbery, have you found yourself believing that the world is unsafe or that you can’t trust people?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, I know bad things can happen, obviously, but I don't walk around thinking everyone is out to get me or that the whole world is completely unsafe. I still trust the people I know, and I don't think everyone is bad just because of what those guys did.\n\nDoctor: When something reminds you of the robbery, does your body react strongly—like your heart pounding, sweating, shaking, or feeling short of breath?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't really noticed anything like that. I mean, my heart doesn't start pounding or anything, and I don't get sweaty or start shaking. Physically, I just feel normal when it comes to mind.\n\nDoctor: When you’re reminded of the robbery, do you ever feel as if it’s actually happening again in that moment?\n\nPatient: Yeah, sometimes. Like if I hear a cash box snapping shut, or... if somebody comes up behind me in a kitchen, it brings the robbery back so fast. I can actually hear the voices again, and my stomach just drops, like it's happening right then in that exact moment.\n\nDoctor: Since the robbery, have you had trouble falling asleep or staying asleep?\n\nPatient: No, my sleep has actually been pretty normal. I don't really have any trouble falling asleep, and I usually stay asleep through the night. So, no, that hasn't been an issue for me.\n\nDoctor: Since the robbery, have you been more irritable or prone to angry outbursts?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I have. Little things just set me off now. Like, if my mom asks me about finding work, or if someone helping me out is moving too slow... I just hear myself snapping at them before I can even stop it. It's like I have no patience anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do these feelings seem tied mainly to the robbery, or do they also happen independently of thinking about it?\n\nPatient: I... I'm not really sure, honestly. I haven't really noticed if I'm thinking about the robbery right when it happens. It just... I just get mad. So I don't really know if it's tied to that specifically, or if it's just happening on its own.\n\nDoctor: Since the robbery, have you had trouble concentrating?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I have. Like, when I try to work on my catering forms or this Chamber application... I just end up reading the same line over and over again. And then I get completely stuck on these really small choices, like what price to put down for something, or trying to figure out which email to answer first. It's just really hard to focus on what I'm doing.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing.
